Ada drew in a long, shaky breath, lifting her head to meet his unreadable gaze. She forced her voice to remain steady, despite the rapid pounding of her heart against her ribs.

"The human skull consists of twenty-two bones, divided into the neurocranium and the viscerocranium," Ada recited, "The eight bones of the neurocranium are the occipital bone, two parietal bones, the frontal bone, two temporal bones, the sphenoid bone, and the ethmoid bone."

"Continue," Wesker said, his voice flat, monitoring her pace.

"The fourteen facial bones of the viscerocranium are the two nasal bones, two maxillae, two lacrimal bones, two zygomatic bones, two palatine bones, two inferior nasal conchae, the vomer, and the mandible," Ada finished, her chest still rising and falling fast, but her words entirely accurate, unhurried, and precise.

Wesker stared down at her for three long seconds. A tiny, almost imperceptible nod touched his head.

"Acceptable," Wesker said smoothly. "Unlace your skates. We are going over your regional music scoring before dinner."

Ada nodded once, her dark eyes sharp with satisfaction as she unlatched the barrier door and stepped off the ice.

─•~❉᯽❉~•─

Sunday mornings in the suburban house on the hill possessed a very specific atmosphere around the precise habits of the man who ran it. There were no lazy lie-ins, no scattered sunday comics spread across the living room carpet, and no lingering scent of burnt toast. Everything moved according to a clear, unwritten schedule that had existed for as long as Ada could remember.

The aroma of frying eggs, cured ham, and fresh espresso drifted up the narrow wooden staircase, cutting through the cool air of the second-floor hallway.

Ada pushed her blanket aside, her small body feeling uncharacteristically stiff and uncooperative. Her head throbbed with a dull, persistent heat behind her eyes, her throat felt as dry as sandpaper, and her eyelids felt swollen and hot. She rubbed her face with her knuckles, letting out a small, raspy sigh before swinging her legs over the side of the mattress.

She stood up, her balance wobbling slightly on the polished hardwood floor, but she smoothed down her dark flannel pyjamas, forced her spine into its usual straight posture, and padded quietly down the stairs in her thick cotton socks.

The kitchen was bathed in the crisp, pale sunlight of early morning. The marble countertops were spotless, gleaming under the bright recessed lights. Standing near the stainless-steel stove, wearing a dark apron over his white button-down shirt with his sleeves rolled neatly to his elbows, was Albert Wesker.

He was currently flipping a round, golden pancake in a small cast-iron skillet with smooth, practiced efficiency. The rhythmic sizzle of the butter was the dominant sound in the warm room.

Ada walked into the kitchen, her steps dragging just a fraction slower than usual. She pulled out a high wooden stool at the kitchen island and sat down, resting her chin in her hands. Her face was flushed pink, her breath coming in quick, shallow puffs through her parted lips.

Wesker didn't turn around immediately, but his voice echoed calmly across the marble island.

"You are four minutes behind schedule, Ada."

"I know," Ada murmured, her small voice raspy, thick, and lacking its usual sharp resonance. "I am sorry."

Wesker paused, his hand holding the handle of the skillet hovering over the burner. The lack of bite in her tone was an immediate anomaly. He turned around slowly, setting the skillet down on the cool element. He lowered his dark sunglasses slightly down the bridge of his nose, his sharp, piercing gaze locking onto her flushed face, taking in the glassy sheen over her dark eyes and the subtle tremor in her small shoulders.

He stepped over to the island, his long strides effortlessly covering the distance. Without saying a word, he extended a large, broad hand, pressing the back of his fingers firmly against her forehead.

His skin was cool against her burning skin.

"Your body temperature is elevated," Wesker stated plainly, his tone devoid of panic, carrying the precise, analytical cadence of a physician reading a laboratory chart. "You have a fever."

"I can still go to training," Ada said quickly, her small throat straining as she forced the words out. "I can do the morning conditioning set. I don't feel that bad."

"Your cognitive assessment is compromised by hyperthermia," Wesker replied smoothly, pulling his hand away and wiping his fingers on a crisp white napkin resting on the counter. "You will not step foot on the ice today."

He reached into his dark trousers pocket, pulled out his black mobile phone, and tapped a brief speed-dial sequence. He held the device to his ear, standing tall and motionless near the island.

The call connected on the second ring. A woman's voice, smooth, slightly distant, and cultured, answered from the speaker.

"Albert?"

"She has a fever," Wesker said smoothly, "Thirty-eight degrees celsius. Cancel her morning ballet block and inform her academic school that she will be absent tomorrow if the temperature persists."

"Is her breathing clear?" the voice asked.

"Shallow, but clear," Wesker answered. "I will manage her hydration and nutrition today. Ensure her schedule for Tuesday is clear as well, as a precautionary measure."

"Understood. I will call her teacher now."

"Good," Wesker said, and tapped the screen, ending the call in less than fifteen seconds. He slipped the phone back into his pocket, turning his dark gaze back down to Ada, who was staring down at the granite countertop with a small, conflicted pout.

"I don't like missing practice," Ada muttered softly, her small fingers tracing the edge of a placemat. "We were supposed to refine the triple toe loop entry today. You said my edge control needed work."

"Your edge control does need work," Wesker corrected smoothly, stepping back toward the stove to turn off the burner under the pan. "However, attempting complex physical maneuvers with impaired reflexes leads to failure and injury. Injury causes a prolonged delay in training. Missing one day is a calculated concession. Missing three months due to a torn ligament is an unacceptable failure of judgment."

Ada stayed silent for a moment, her swollen, glassy eyes tracking his movements as he picked up a spatual and plated a stack of warm, golden pancakes beside a small bowl of sliced strawberries.

"I can still be useful," Ada insisted, her stubbornness flaring through her tiredness. She pushed herself up off the high stool, her socks sliding slightly on the smooth floor as she stood up straight. "If I cannot skate today, I should complete my chores."

Wesker turned around, holding a plate of food in one hand and a glass of fresh orange juice in the other. He looked down at her small, determined figure, taking in her set jaw and her burning cheeks.

"You are sick, Ada," Wesker noted, his tone flat.

"I am not helpless," Ada countered, her small voice firm despite the scratchy tone. "If I am sitting down, I will just think about the ice. Let me do something."

Wesker stared at her for. The soft morning light bounced off the side of his dark glasses, hiding whatever small glimmer of amusement might have passed through his mind. He set the plate and the juice glass down on the island near her seat.

He gestured with a long, leather-gloved finger toward the stainless-steel sink near the window, where a small pile of prep bowls, whisk attachments, and measuring cups rested from his breakfast preparation.

"Very well," Wesker said smoothly. "If you insist on physical activity, you may wash the prep dishes while the breakfast cools. Use the warm water. Do not splash it onto the floor."

"Yes, Coach," Ada said instantly, a small, subtle sense of satisfaction settling over her flushed face.

She dragged a small wooden step stool over from the pantry, placing it squarely in front of the deep stainless-steel sink. She climbed up, her small socks gripping the wooden rungs, and turned on the chrome faucet. Warm steam instantly rose from the basin as the water filled the stainless-steel basin.

Ada picked up a yellow sponge, squeezed a drop of dish soap onto it, and began carefully scrubbing a glass mixing bowl.The warm water felt incredible against her chilled, feverish hands, soothing the dull ache in her joints as she worked.

Behind her, the kitchen was filled with the clean, pleasant sounds of his continued work. Wesker moved smoothly around the space, opening the refrigerator to retrieve fresh butter, pouring two cups of rich, dark coffee into porcelain mugs, and arranging utensils on two woven placemats with neat, parallel alignment.

"The water temperature is acceptable?" Wesker asked from near the stove, his back turned to her as he checked the temperature of a small saucepan of syrup.

"It is warm," Ada replied softly, her small hands thoroughly rinsing the soap off a metal whisk before placing it neatly on the drying rack beside the sink. "It feels good."

"Ensure the soap residue is entirely removed from the glassware," Wesker instructed calmly, not turning around. "Chemical contamination alters the taste of future preparations."

"I am being thorough," Ada noted, picking up a second bowl and scrubbing the inner curve with precise, circular motions.

For ten minutes, the steady flow of warm water from the faucet, the soft clink of glass meeting the drying rack, and the sizzle of breakfast items on the stovetop created a peaceful, grounded sanctuary against the cold morning outside.

Ada felt her feverish head throbbing slightly, but the simple, repetitive task kept her focused. She washed every cup, every spoon, and every small prep dish until the sink was completely empty and the drying rack was filled with sparkling, perfectly clean kitchenware.

She turned off the faucet, dried her small hands thoroughly on a clean hand towel, and stepped down from the wooden stool.

Wesker had already finished setting the island. Two plates rested on the placemats, each holding a neat stack of pancakes, three slices of crispy bacon, and a small portion of fresh fruit. Beside Ada’s plate rested her glass of orange juice along with two small, white fever- reducing tablets setting on a folded paper napkin.

Wesker stood beside his own stool, his apron removed and folded neatly over the back of a chair. He looked down at the empty sink, then at the neatly organized drying rack, inspecting her work with a single, sweeping glance.

"Acceptable work," Wesker said smoothly. An approval that meant more to Ada than a dozen loud praises from school teachers. "The glass is clear."

"Thank you," Ada murmured, climbing back up onto her high wooden stool.

"Take the medication first," Wesker ordered, gesturing toward the white tablets. "Then consume the meal. You require caloric intake to fight the infection."

─•~❉᯽❉~•─

Thirteen was a strange, fragile age to realize that the adults who governed your world were merely human beings trapped inside their own carefully constructed cages.

Ada stood frozen in the dark corridor outside the master bedroom suite, her small feet clad in soft, lacey white socks that dug gently into the thick pile of the rug. She wore a simple light blue baby doll dress, its cotton fabric loose around her shoulders, the hem falling just above her knees.

Her fingers were clasped tightly together in front of her chest, her knuckles turning pale as she pressed herself flat against the cool, painted drywall.

Inside the dimly lit room, the argument had been building for nearly twenty minutes. "Let go of me, Albert," Eleonor hissed, her voice dripping with venom.

Ada crept two inches closer to the cracked door frame, her heart hammered against her ribs. She pulled her knees up toward her chest, crouching low against the baseboard, wrapping her slender arms around her lace-clad shins to make herself as small as humanly possible.

Through the narrow slit in the doorway, Ada could see them clearly under the amber glow of the bedside lamp.

Eleonor was standing near the foot of the mahogany bed, her posture stiff, her high-end silk robe crumpled around her waist. Her face was contorted into bitter, burning resentment. She had reached out, her fingers twisting violently into the front of Wesker's dark grey pajama shirt, her manicured nails digging through the soft fabric as she shoved him backward with all her strength.

Wesker did not stumble.

He stood like a massive pillar in the center of the bedroom, his broad shoulders squared, his blond hair uncharacteristically messy from sleep. He was not wearing his dark glasses, leaving his pale, intense eyes completely exposed in the dim light.

"Eleonor, please," Wesker requested, his tone fragile and vulnerable that Ada had never heard in her entire life. "Listen to reason. You need to be here. She is thirteen years old now. She needs her mother too. You have to be there for our daughter."

"Our daughter?" Eleonor spat, her voice rising into a mocking screech that made Ada flinch against the wall. She shoved him again, her small fists balling up the cotton of his pajamas. "Don't you dare lay that emotional guilt on me, Albert! You were the one who wanted her! You were the one who insisted on having a child! I never wanted a baby ruining my career!"

Wesker's hands hovered near her elbows, hesitating, almost begging her to stop. "Eleonor, I have been doing my absolute best. I spend every waking hour keeping Ada from wondering why you aren't here. I keep her from worrying about why her mother never sits down for dinner, why you don't come to her recitals, why you don't care about her ice skating. I am begging you, just give her ten minutes of your time."

"I am done pretending!" Eleonor screamed, letting go of his shirt so hard the fabric snapped back against his chest. She took a step backward, waving her hand dismissively through the air as if sweeping away a pile of dust. "I am sick of this! I am sick of coming back to this house and looking at that child!"

Wesker's pale eyes widened slightly, a flicker of shock crossing his features. "Eleonor, stop. Do not say that."

"Why shouldn't I say it?!" Eleonor shrieked, her voice echoing off the high ceiling. "I hate her, Albert! I loathe coming through that front door! She looks at me with those eyes, and all I see is a burden! I loathe her!"

Ada’s breath caught squarely in her throat. She pulled her knees tighter to her chest, burying her face into the light blue cotton of her baby doll dress. The fabric felt cool against her burning cheeks. Her small, lacey socks slid an inch against the carpet as she curled herself into a tight, defensive ball in the dark hallway.

"She is a child, Eleonor!" Wesker pleaded softly, his voice trembling with a suppressed, painful strain as he took a step toward his wife. "She is brilliant, she is disciplined, and she loves you. I am doing everything I can to raise her right. I am doing my best to manage this house so you can focus on your work, but you have to give us something!"

"I don't want a family, Albert!" Eleonor screamed back, her hands tearing through her own hair in frustration. "I wanted a career! I wanted a normal, unencumbered life without a husband playing homemaker and a child expecting me to cradle her needs! I didn't even want to be married to you!"

The words hung in the air like a suffocating fog.

Wesker went completely still. His broad shoulders dropped half an inch, his face turning entirely pale under the warm lamp light. "You... you don't mean that, Eleonor. You're exhausted from the lab. You're stressed about the grant approval. You don't mean that."

"I DO MEAN IT!" Eleonor roared, her voice cracking with malice. "I have hated every single day of this marriage! You and that girl are nothing but chains around my neck, dragging me down into your pathetic mundane existence!"

She turned on her heel, marching toward the walk-in closet. She snatched a leather travel bag off the top shelf, throwing it onto the bed with violent, careless force.

Wesker took two fast strides forward, his hands reaching out toward her shoulders, his voice rising in genuine panic. "Eleonor, where are you going?!"

"Away!" she screamed, yanking a stack of clothes off the hangers and stuffing them into the bag without bothering to fold them. "I am leaving! I am taking the university placement in Switzerland! I am done with this house, I am done with this marriage, and I am done with you!"

"Eleonor, please! Think about Ada!" Wesker begged, his fingers brushing her arm as he tried to stop her from zipping the leather bag. "You can't just walk out in the middle of the night! What am I supposed to tell her in the morning?!"

"Tell her whatever you want!" Eleonor barked, yanking the bag off the bed and slinging the leather strap over her shoulder. She shoved past him, her eyes cold, hard, and devoid of love. "Tell her I died! Tell her I vanished! I do not care!"

She marched straight out of the bedroom, her hard heels slapping loudly against the wooden threshold before hitting the hallway runner.

Ada shrank back into the corner, pressing her back as flat as possible against the wall, her blue baby doll dress pooling around her lace-clad ankles. She kept her head down, her small body trembling as Eleonor swept past her without a single glance, without a pause, and without a second of hesitation.

The front door on the ground floor opened with a click, followed by the slam of solid oak meeting the frame. The roar of a car engine started up in the driveway, its headlights casting long, sweeping beams of light through the living room windows before fading into the cold, empty distance.

Then, absolute silence settled over the house.

Wesker stood in the doorway of the master bedroom, his dark grey pajamas rumpled, his hair messy, his broad frame looking strangely small in the dim light. His hands were clenched into tight fists at his sides, his head bowed, his shoulders rising and falling in slow, heavy breaths.

Ada slowly lowered her legs, her lacey socks making a tiny, whispering sound against the carpet as she stood up. Her small blue dress hung straight down, her short dark hair falling slightly forward over her eyes.

She took quiet, hesitant steps down the dark corridor.

"Coach?" Ada called out softly, her small voice raspy, fragile, and breaking the silence of the house.

Wesker's head snapped up instantly.

He looked toward the sound of her voice, his pale eyes catching her small, delicate figure standing in the dim hallway. He quickly pulled his hands out of his fists, smoothing down the front of his grey pajama shirt, trying his best to pull his face back into its usual calm, stoic mask.

"Ada," Wesker said, his voice dropping into its low register, though a faint, barely noticeable raspy edge remained. He took two steps toward her, his long strides closing the distance. "Why are you out of bed? What are you doing here?"

Ada looked down at her white lacey socks, her fingers twisting the hem of her light blue baby doll dress. "I couldn't sleep," she whispered softly. "I heard shouting."

Wesker reached her side and simply sank down onto the carpeted floor, dropping to one knee so he was positioned precisely at her eye level.

He looked into her dark, clear eyes, taking in her small, pale face, her trembling shoulders, and the understanding resting in her gaze. He reached out with his broad, warm hand, gently resting his large palm against the side of her shoulder, holding her steady.

Ada looked into his face, "Is my mommy coming back?" she asked softly.

Wesker stared at her. The warm hall light caught the pale, unreadable depths of his eyes. He knew what Eleonor had said. He knew the hatred, the bitterness, and the finality of the front door slamming shut. He knew she was never coming back.

Wesker lifted his hand, his thumb gently wiping away a single, stray drop of moisture near the corner of Ada's eye.

"Yes," Albert lied smoothly as he held her gaze. "She will be back. Now go to sleep, Ada."

─•~❉᯽❉~•─

Wesker lied.

It was a simple, flat truth that settled into the foundation of the house like dust on an unread book. Her mother never came back after that night. There were no surprise phone calls, no lingering letters arriving in the mail, and no sudden returns in the middle of the afternoon. Her mother simply ceased to exist within the walls of their lives, erased as cleanly as a miscalculated equation on a chalkboard.

By the time Ada turned fifteen, her life had been engineered into a precise machine of perpetual movement.

Her daily schedule was a grueling marathon that would have broken most adults. She rose at four in the morning for two hours of advanced gymnastics. By eight, she was in the classroom, maintaining an flawless academic record that kept her firmly at the very top of her class rank. At three in the afternoon, she was at the ballet barre, perfecting her posture, turnout, and balance under strict classical instruction. By six in the evening, she was on the ice, her steel blades carving deep arcs through the frozen surface until the arena lights flickered off for the night.

Wesker was there for every single practice.

"Your arm placement was low on the third leap," Wesker said as Ada stepped off the gymnastics mat as he looked down at his clipboard. "And your turn lacked elevation. You are losing height on your jump."

Ada wiped a thin sheen of sweat from her forehead with a small towel, her breath coming in controlled cycles. "I will correct the height on the next pass."

"You need to correct it now," Wesker replied, his tone lacking any warmth,"The regional qualifiers are in three weeks. Flaws in fundamental form will cost you points."

"I understand," Ada said smoothly, her chin lifted.

"Go change," Wesker instructed, checking his watch. "We have twenty minutes before the ice rink opens across the street."

Ada nodded once and headed down the long, tiled corridor toward the secondary changing rooms near the back of the athletic facility. The hallway was dim, the locker doors painted a dull navy blue.

As she rounded the corner near the showers, the muffled sound of harsh voices echoing off the wet tiles made her pause.

"Give it back!" a shrill, frightened girl's voice cried out, accompanied by a wet thud against the locker doors. "Leave me alone! That's my bag!"

"Shut up," a boy's rough voice snapped back. "Who's gonna make us? Nobody comes down this hallway after five."

"Yeah, cry about it, Graham," a second boy sneered, followed by the loud clatter of metal lockers being slammed shut. "Maybe your rich dad can buy you a new bag."

Ada didn't hesitate.

She stepped smoothly around the corner, her dark eyes locking onto three older boys who had backed a small blonde girl against the metal lockers. The girl was trembling, her hands clutching her bruised elbow, tears streaming down her flushed cheeks.

"Leave her alone," Ada said, her voice dropping into a sharp register.

The three boys turned, looking at the slender fifteen-year-old girl standing in the doorway in her dark training clothes.

"Get lost, shorty," the largest boy scoffed, taking a step toward her. "This doesn't involve you."

"I said," Ada repeated softly, her posture shifting as her center of gravity dropped into a balanced stance, "leave her alone."

The boy lunged forward to grab her shoulder, but Ada moved swiftly away. She dodged his reach, caught his wrist, and twisted his arm backward with sharp, leverage-based force. The boy let out a pained yell, stumbling sideways into his friend.

The second boy threw an wild, uncoordinated punch that grazed Ada’s face, his knuckles clipping her bottom lip with enough force to split the soft skin against her teeth. Warm blood instantly welled up over her lip, dripping down her chin.

Ada didn't even wince. The dull sting only sharpened her focus. She drove her elbow hard into the second boy's ribs, sending him gasping to the floor, before sweeping the third boy's legs out from under him with a quick, decisive kick to his ankle.

In less than thirty seconds, all three boys were scrambling off the wet floor tiles, cursing, clutching their bruises, and retreating down the hallway as fast as their legs could carry them.

Silence returned to the locker room, broken only by the soft, ragged sound of the blonde girl's crying.

The girl stumbled forward, pulling a clean white handkerchief from her pocket with trembling hands. She reached up, her small fingers shaking violently as she pressed the soft cloth gently against Ada's busted, bleeding lip.

"Oh my gosh," the girl sobbed softly, tears brimming in her wide blue eyes as she held the handkerchief steady. "You're bleeding! I'm so sorry! They were taking my cheer uniform and my bag, and I didn't know what to do!"

"Hold it still," Ada instructed calmly, taking the handkerchief from her hand and pressing it against her lip herself. "The bleeding will stop in a minute."

Footsteps echoed sharply down the hallway.

Wesker stepped around the corner, his long coat swishing against his boots. His dark eyes swept over the wet floor tiles, the disheveled lockers, the crying blonde girl, and finally settled on his daughter.

A sharp flicker of cold anger crossed his features. His brow furrowed into a deep, stern line as he stepped into the changing room, looking down at the blood soaking into the white cloth pressed to her mouth.

"Ada," Wesker said, his voice dropping into a harsh, stern tone. "What is this? Why are you bleeding?"

"It was an altercation," Ada replied smoothly, keeping her voice level despite the swelling pain in her mouth. "Some athletes were harassing her."

"I don't care about other athletes," Wesker snapped, "You have a session in ten minutes. Look at your face. Your lip is split."

"It's just a minor cut, Coach," Ada said, stepping back from the locker.

"It is a distraction," Wesker corrected sternly, checking his watch again with an irritated flick of his wrist. "You are wasting time on things that do not concern your training. Clean your face immediately and get to the car."

He turned on his heel without another word, his boots clicking sharply against the tile as he walked back out into the main corridor, leaving the two girls standing in the dim room.

The blonde girl looked at Ada with wide, awe-struck eyes, her tears drying as she watched Ada calmly dab the last drops of blood from her chin.

As Ada turned to gather her training bag and head toward the exit, the blonde girl took a quick step forward, calling out after her.

"Wait!" the girl pleaded, her voice ringing out clear against the navy lockers. "Please! What's your name?"

Ada paused near the doorway. She turned her head slightly, her dark eyes looking back over her shoulder at the blonde girl.

"Ada," she answered smoothly, her voice calm and unbothered. "Ada Wong."

─•~❉᯽❉~•─

Ada stood perfectly still near the stage left entrance, holding a slender black microphone in her hand. She was wearing a crisp white collared shirt, tailored charcoal pants, and a dark blazer neatly fitted to her frame, representing the school publication for the annual Founder’s Day live broadcast.

Standing right in front of her, completely ignoring the surrounding noise of the technical crew, was her father.

On his broad left shoulder hung a massive, bright pink Hello Kitty tote bag. The vibrant cartoon character printed on the canvas was a stark, almost absurd against his dark coat, but he carried it like a badge of honor. Inside the spacious bag were three freshly laundered changes of clothes, a comprehensive first aid kit, four insulated water bottles kept at specific temperatures, and three folded cotton towels.

"Stop leaning your head back," Wesker instructed calmly, his voice flat and unhurried as his large hands reached up to gently adjust the small wire of her earpiece behind her ear. "If the clip rubs against your cartilage, it will irritate the skin before the broadcast concludes."

"It was slipping," Ada noted, keeping her chin level as he secured the wire flush against her jawline.

"It was loose because you adjusted it incorrectly," Wesker replied smoothly. He reached into his coat pocket, pulled out a small, clean cotton towel, and carefully slid it down the back of her shirt collar to absorb any stray moisture while she stood under the hot stage lights.

Without pausing, he reached into his other pocket and produced two shiny red hair clips. He unfastened them with practiced ease, sliding her neat dark hair back from her forehead and securing the strands smoothly on either side of her head. He reached up, his long fingers carefully straightening the crisp points of her white collar, making sure not a single crease showed on camera.

"Look down," Wesker ordered softly.

Ada lowered her gaze as Wesker crouched down on one knee right in front of her. His broad frame occupied half the narrow hallway, but his large hands moved with meticulous care as he untied the laces of her polished black leather shoes, pulled the tongue straight, and retied the knots with firm, double-bow precision. He then took the hem of her charcoal pants, smoothing the fabric down so it fell in a clean, unbroken line over the tops of her footwear.

"Your schedule is shifting starting next Monday," Wesker stated, his voice even and matter-of- fact as he stood back up, adjusting the strap of the pink Hello Kitty bag on his shoulder.

Ada blinked, her dark eyes flicking up to his face. "My schedule is already full. I already have —"

"I am aware of your commitments," Wesker replied, pulling a small notebook from his pocket and checking his notes. "I enrolled you in private oil painting on Tuesday and Thursday afternoons. On Saturday mornings, before your ballet bar, you will have two hours of classical piano instruction."

"Painting and piano?" Ada asked, a small line forming between her dark eyebrows. "Coach, I am already managing top academic marks and three sports."

"A well-rounded profile requires artistic discipline alongside physical excellence," Wesker said flatly, "The university admissions boards look for artistic mastery. Painting will refine your spatial awareness. Piano will improve your finger dexterity and rhythm."

"When am I supposed to study for advanced history?" Ada questioned smoothly, keeping her voice low so the nearby stage manager wouldn't hear.

"You will study between seven and eight in the morning, before your first class," Wesker answered without hesitation. "I have adjusted your meal preparation times accordingly. Your breakfast will be ready at six-thirty instead of seven."

Ada looked at him for two seconds, processing the revised routine in her mind. She didn't complain, nor did she argue. She simply nodded once, accepting the new structure as she had accepted every instruction before it. "I will manage it."

"See that you do," Wesker said, unzipping the top compartment of the Hello Kitty bag. He pulled out one of the insulated water bottles, unscrewed the cap, and held it out toward her. "Take two sips. No more than that. You do not want to look bloated on the monitor."

Ada took the bottle, drank two measured swallows, and handed it back. Wesker took it, screwed the cap shut with a firm twist, and stowed it back into the pink bag beside the first aid kit.

"Ten seconds to live coverage, Ada!" the student director yelled from across the backstage area, pointing a finger toward her.

Wesker stepped back, taking a position near the dark curtains where he could observe the monitor screen. His face remained calm and distant.

"Keep your posture straight, Ada," Wesker added quietly, his voice carrying clearly over the countdown. "Speak slowly, articulate every consonant, and do not slouch."

"Yes, Dad," Ada murmured softly, adjusting her grip on the microphone.

Wesker didn't smile, but his pale eyes remained fixed on her as the red recording light on the main camera flickered to life.

─•~❉᯽❉~•─

Six gold medals in a single competitive season was a feat that sports commentators openly declared statistical insanity. Three gold medals on the ice for figure skating—the short program, the free skate, and the overall individual crown—followed by three gold medals on the wooden floors for elite gymnastics in the ribbon, hoop, and all-around categories.

As if to seal her total dominance over the athletic and performing arts divisions in a single sweep, her dual performance as Odette and Odile in the municipal conservatory’s production of Swan Lake had sold out its entire six-night run in less than forty minutes.

The press room behind the main arena was a humid, bright furnace of noise, blinding white halogen bulbs, and clicking camera shutters.

Dozens of reporters, sports journalists, and flash photography crews were crammed behind the velvet ropes, their voices overlapping in a desperate cacophony of questions. Microphones wrapped in foam windscreens bearing the logos of every major state network were thrust forward like a thick forest of plastic branches.

When the side door opened, the blinding white flashes went completely wild, illuminating the room in rapid, strobe-like bursts of light.

Ada stepped smoothly up to the small wooden podium.

At sixteen years old, she moved with an unshakeable, hypnotic composure that completely belied her age. She was dressed in a striking crimson baby doll dress, the rich red fabric draping loosely around her slender frame, the hem ending neatly above her knees. On her feet were dark red leather Mary Jane heels paired with delicate, pristine white lacy socks that covered her ankles. Her dark hair was cut into her signature sleek bob, and her red-framed glasses caught the sharp glare of the flashing press cameras as she folded her hands neatly on the wooden stand.

Wesker stood precisely three feet to her left, slightly behind the podium. He wore his classic dark coat, his arms crossed over his broad chest, his dark glasses reflecting the relentless strobe of the flashbulbs like two polished obsidian mirrors.

The lead reporter from the state athletic journal practically leaped forward, pressing his recorder close to the microphone bank.

"Ada! Over here! Ada, six gold medals in less than two weeks!" the reporter shouted over the din. "You swept the entire figure skating roster, shattered the national scoring record for gymnastics, and performed the principal lead in Swan Lake all in the same fortnight! How does it feel to achieve total perfection at sixteen?"

Ada adjusted her posture slightly, leaning forward toward the cluster of microphones, unbothered by the flashing light.

"It feels satisfactory," Ada replied, "The preparation was thorough, and the execution aligned with our established targets."

A female journalist from the regional arts gazette pushed her way toward the front, holding a notepad. "Ada! The dance critics are calling your Odette the most technically precise interpretation seen in a decade! How did you manage to balance the emotional vulnerability of the White Swan with the grueling physical demand of your figure skating routine?"

"Odette is a study in balance," Ada answered smoothly, her dark eyes looking directly into the lens of the primary news camera. "The emotional output is simply an extension of correct muscular tension. If the form is accurate, the emotion presents itself naturally."

"That's all?" the journalist pressed, looking surprised by the brevity of the response. "No secret ritual? No artistic inspiration?"

"No," Ada said simply, offering a tiny, perfectly polite nod. "Precision does not require a ritual."

Another reporter waved his hand frantically from the second row. "Ada! The national Olympic development squad has already confirmed they are sending scouts to your next regional meet! Are you intending to commit to the international skating circuit full time, or will you continue to pursue professional ballet and gymnastics simultaneously?"

Ada didn't glance at her father. She kept her chin lifted, her small hands resting light and steady against the edge of the wooden podium.

"My schedule is managed on a weekly basis," Ada stated, "We evaluate opportunities as they present themselves. Our focus remains on the immediate workload."

"Is it true your training load exceeds eight hours a day?" a reporter from the back row called out. "People are saying your routine is inhuman!"

Ada tilted her head slightly, the red frames of her glasses reflecting the blue indicator lights of the video cameras.

"My routine is structured," Ada corrected calmly. "Human potential expands according to discipline. There is nothing inhuman about proper preparation."

"What about your personal life, Ada?" a young local reporter asked with a warm, probing smile. "You are top of your class academically, a multi-gold medalist, a prima ballerina, and a school journalist. Do you ever just go out with friends? Do you have time to be a normal teenager?"

The press room went briefly quiet, waiting for a crack in the pristine, flawless facade of the teenage prodigy.

Wesker's dark glasses turned two inches to the left, his focus locking onto the young reporter who asked the question, but he did not speak. He let his daughter handle the floor.

Ada offered the room a small, elegant curve of her lips, a look that was polite, pleasant, and completely impenetrable.

"My personal life is adequately balanced," Ada replied, her voice remaining low, smooth, and perfectly controlled. "I enjoy my studies, I enjoy my sports, and I value my results. That is entirely sufficient for me."

"Do you have anything to say to the young athletes who look up to you as a role model?" the lead reporter shouted, raising his camera for another rapid barrage of flashes. "Any message for the regional field before the upcoming winter games?"

Ada looked straight into the central camera lens, her dark eyes clear, and intensely focused behind her red-framed glasses.

"Work hard," Ada said softly. "The ice does not care about excuses, and the judges do not reward sentiment. Prepare properly, or do not compete at all."

She didn't wait for another question. She didn't offer a final wave, nor did she linger for the reporters who were already screaming her name for follow-up statements.

Ada stepped neatly away from the wooden podium, her dark red Mary Jane heels clicking softly against the polished floor, her lacey socks neat around her ankles. Wesker stepped smoothly in front of her, his large frame effortlessly clearing a path through the crowded exit corridor as the flashbulbs continued to pop in a frantic, endless white storm behind them.

They walked down the long rear hallway of the facility, away from the noise, the press, and the blinding lights. The air here was cool, smelling faintly of clean floor wax and distant ice. Wesker checked his silver wristwatch with a brief, efficient movement as they reached the private parking exit.

"Your interview responses were acceptable," Wesker noted casually, his low voice echoing off the concrete walls. "Short. Polite. You gave them no material to construct unnecessary narratives."

"They ask the same questions every season," Ada noted softly, smoothing down the front of her crimson baby doll dress as they walked side by side toward the sleek black sedan parked near the bay doors. "They look for drama where there is only mechanical repetition."

"Let them look," Wesker replied flatly, pulling the car key from his coat pocket. "Public curiosity is irrelevant. Your second ballet rehearsal for the regional showcase begins at seven tomorrow morning. We need to leave the house by six fifteen."

"I am aware," Ada said as she reached for the passenger door handle. "My bags are already packed."

─•~❉᯽❉~•─

Some people learned to ice skate because they fell in love with the effortless glide across a frozen surface. Others learned because their parents pushed them onto the ice before they could even walk properly.

Ashley Graham learned to ice skate for one reason and one reason only. Ada Wong.

It had started as a silly, impulsive decision three months after Ada had saved her from those track athletes in the locker room. Ashley had bought a pair of white leather figure skates, hired a private instructor for three grueling sessions a week, and spent every free hour after cheer practice falling flat on her face until her knees were permanently bruised a deep, mottled purple. She didn't care about medals, she didn't care about routines, and she certainly didn't care about technical edge scores. She simply wanted an excuse to be inside the rink when Ada was training, to belong in the same cold space that Ada occupied with such breathtaking, isolated perfection.

The main ice arena was freezing at seven o'clock on a Tuesday evening.

The high overhead floodlights were turned down to half power, casting a cool, blue-tinted luminescence across the pristine sheet of fresh ice. The public sessions had ended hours ago, leaving the vast building silent except for the hum of the refrigeration units humming deep beneath the concrete floor.

Ada was on the ice alone.

It was a rare occurrence. Wesker had been called away to a mandatory faculty meeting at the university research center, leaving Ada with a precious ninety-minute window of unmonitored practice time. Most athletes would have used the rare absence of their coach to rest their aching muscles or leave early. Ada simply used it to drill her inner edge transitions over and over, her steel blades carving sharp, precise circles into the glass surface.

She was dressed in a dark grey thermal long-sleeve top and tight black training pants, her hair tied back into a sleek, neat ponytail. She glided across the center circle, her posture perfectly erect, her dark eyes focused on her blade tracks.

From the wooden bench near the rink entrance, the sound of white leather skates clacking against the rubber floor mats broke her concentration.

Ada brought herself to a smooth, spraying stop near the barrier, turning her head.

Ashley was clinging onto the wooden handrail for dear life, her white skates wobbling beneath her as she carefully navigated the rubber matting. She wore a bright yellow oversized sweater, dark blue leggings, and a fluffy white knit beanie pulled down over her ears. Her cheeks were flushed bright pink from the chill of the arena, her blue eyes sparkling with nervous excitement.

"You're going to break an ankle if you step on the ice with your laces that loose, Ashley," Ada noted softly, resting her gloved hands on the top rail of the wooden barrier.

Ashley paused, gripping the rail tighter, a wide, sheepish grin breaking across her face. "I tied them as tight as I could! I pulled until my fingers went completely numb!"

"Bring your foot up," Ada instructed smoothly.

Ashley carefully lifted her right skate, resting the blade on the edge of the barrier. Ada reached down, her gloved fingers catching the thick white laces. With three swift, expert tugs, she tightened the crisscross pattern, securing the knot with precise, firm tension before doing the exact same to Ashley's left skate.

"There," Ada said. "Now you won't wobble."

"Thanks," Ashley breathed out, letting out a small, relieved sigh. She pushed open the small wooden access door and stepped onto the ice. Her blades wobbled for a single second before she found her balance, gliding a short feet toward Ada with her arms extended wide like a tightrope walker. "See? I'm getting better! My coach said I'm almost ready to attempt spin entries!"

Ada tilted her head, a small, subtle curve touching the corner of her lips as she observed the blonde girl's awkward, enthusiastic stance. "Spin entries require proper core engagement, Ashley. You are leaning too far forward on your toes."

"I am not!" Ashley protested playfully, taking two deliberate, sweeping strides toward the center of the rink. "Look, I've been practicing the basic twirl all week! I watched a video tutorial twenty times!"

"A video tutorial is not a substitute for proper technique," Ada cautioned, turning her body to glide alongside her, keeping a watchful two-foot distance.

"Watch me!" Ashley declared with stubborn determination.

Ashley set her left blade into a wide curve, attempting to pull her arms inward to initiate a basic two-foot spin. She pushed off with her right skate, but her weight was completely misaligned. Instead of centering her force over the ball of her foot, she caught the sharp toe pick of her left blade directly into the smooth surface of the ice.

Click.

The abrupt friction instantly halted her momentum. Ashley’s legs buckled under her, her balance evaporating completely as her body pitched forward toward the hard, unforgiving floor.

"Whoa!" Ashley shrieked, her hands flailing wildly in the cold air.

In a fraction of a second, before Ashley’s knees could slam into the ice, Ada moved.

Ada drove her blades deep into the ice, crossing the distance between them in an instant. She extended her arms, catching Ashley securely around her waist. She absorbed the full force of Ashley's forward momentum, her own skates gliding backward across the surface in a long, controlled curve to keep them both upright.

The sudden movement brought them to a complete, drifting stop near the center red line.

Ashley gasped, her hands instinctively wrapping tightly around Ada's shoulders to anchor herself. Her chest rose and fell in rapid, terrified cycles, her heart pounding frantically against her ribs from the near-fall.

They were standing impossibly close.

Ada’s hands were locked firmly around Ashley’s lower back, holding her steady against her frame. Ashley’s face was mere inches away, her breath rising in warm, soft white clouds in the freezing air between them. The bright yellow knit of Ashley’s sweater brushed against the dark grey fabric of Ada’s thermal top.

"I told you," Ada murmured softly, her low, smooth voice vibrating through the small space separating them. "Your weight was on your toe pick."

Ashley didn't reply immediately. Her blue eyes, wide with lingering adrenaline, shifted from Ada's dark, clear eyes down to her soft, rosy lips, and then back up again. The cool chill of the arena seemed to evaporate entirely, replaced by a sudden, sweet, suffocating heat that filled the space between their chests.

"You caught me," Ashley whispered, her voice raspy, small, and trembling slightly.

"I always catch you," Ada replied, her tone dropping into a low register that sent a shiver straight down Ashley's spine.

Ashley closed the tiny gap remaining between their faces, leaning forward to press her lips directly against Ada's.

The kiss was soft, sweet, and entirely sudden.

Ada’s breath hitched in her throat, her body freezing for a moment under the unexpected touch. But as Ashley’s gloved fingers tangled into the fabric near Ada’s neck, pulling her closer, Ada didn't step back. She didn't push her away. Slowly, deliberately, Ada relaxed her posture, her hands tightening around Ashley’s waist, leaning into the warmth of the kiss as they floated together on the cold mirror of the ice.

The soft touch lasted for long breathtaking seconds before Ashley pulled back slightly, her cheeks burning a bright, brilliant crimson, her breath coming in quick, shallow puffs.

She looked into Ada's dark eyes, panic suddenly flaring in her chest. "I... I'm sorry, Ada! I didn't mean to just—"

"Ashley," Ada interrupted gently. She reached up with one gloved hand, her fingers gently brushing a stray lock of blonde hair back behind Ashley's ear. "Stop rambling."

Ashley swallowed hard, a tiny, helpless smile breaking across her flushed face. "Okay."

A double beep from an electronic key fob echoed loudly across the wide, empty arena from the main entrance doors.

The glass doors swung open, and Wesker stepped into the facility, his tall frame clad in his dark trench coat, his boots clicking sharply against the rubber floor mats as he walked toward the wooden barrier.

Ada smoothly released her hold on Ashley's waist, stepping back a full foot onto a clean edge, her expression instantly shifting back into its usual cool, disciplined composure.

Ashley scrambled to regain her balance, her face still burning hot as she turned toward the barrier. "Hi, Mr. Wesker!" she called out, her voice squeaking slightly with nervous energy.

Albert didn't smile. He stopped at the wooden barrier, resting his gloved hands on the top rail as his dark glasses swept over the two girls standing on the ice. He took in Ashley's bright red cheeks, Ada's perfectly composed stance, and the slight, lingering disturbance in the ice tracks where they had been standing together.

"It is eight thirty, Ada," Wesker announced, his voice flat, calm, and distant, echoing off the high ceiling. "Your practice time has concluded."

"Yes, Coach," Ada replied smoothly, turning her blades toward the exit door.

"Do you need a ride home, Miss Graham?" Wesker offered, his tone polite and formal."The night air is dropping below freezing."

"Oh! Um, yes please!" Ashley said quickly, carefully gliding toward the wooden door. "Thank you so much!"

Ten minutes later, Ada sat quietly in the front passenger seat of Wesker's sleek black sedan.

Her hair was brushed neatly, her dark coat buttoned up to her throat, her hands resting folded in her lap. In the back seat, Ashley had already fallen fast asleep, exhausted from hours of cheer drills and her chaotic skating attempts, her head resting against the cold leather interior.

The car moved smoothly down the dark, quiet highway, the street lamps casting long beams of amber light across the dashboard.

Wesker kept both hands on the steering wheel, his eyes fixed on the empty road ahead. "You are distracted tonight, Ada," He stated plainly.

Ada didn't flinch. She kept her gaze looking straight out the windshield at the dark road. "My edge work was consistent, Father."

"Your edge work was acceptable," Wesker corrected smoothly, "Your focus, however, was divided."

Ada stayed silent, her jaw setting in a subtle line.

Wesker adjusted his grip on the leather steering wheel, his dark glasses catching the passing yellow streetlights.

"I am aware of your fondness for Miss Graham," Wesker continued, his tone carrying no anger, no judgment, and no emotional weight whatsoever, "She is a pleasant girl, and her family holds significant social standing in the state capital."

He paused for a single beat, letting his next words settle cleanly into the air of the vehicle.

"However, if you intend to keep her around you," Albert added calmly, his voice steady and firm, "you will not allow her to become a distraction to your training. Your athletic credentials and your academic performance remain your primary objectives. Do not let minor personal entanglements compromise your discipline."

Ada looked out the side window, watching the dark trees blur past in the night. She listened to the soft breathing of the blonde girl sleeping peacefully in the back seat, feeling the cool, solid weight of her father's presence sitting beside her.

"I understand, Father," Ada replied softly, her voice smooth, calm, and entirely unwavering. "It will not happen again."

"Good," Albert said flatly, maintaining his steady speed as the black car glided smoothly through the dark night.

─•~❉᯽❉~•─

To be known by someone is a strange, delicate thing, but to be watched by someone who refuses to look away is something else entirely.

Since the day they kissed, every single afternoon at four o'clock sharp, the glass doors of the arena would swing open, and Ashley would shuffle down the rubber matting in her oversized yellow cheerleader jacket, carrying a large thermos of warm tea and a brightly colored poster board.

She sat three rows up in the empty bleachers, directly behind the wooden judge's box where Wesker stood.

Ashley knew better than to shout. She had learned early on that loud outbursts earned a cold, sharp turn of Wesker's dark sunglasses, a silent look that could freeze water faster than the refrigeration pipes beneath the floor. So, Ashley adapted. She sat with her knees pulled up to her chin, her blue eyes tracking Ada’s every glide, jump, and spin across the glass surface.

Whenever Ada executed a difficult triple combination, Ashley would sneak a quick, cautious glance at the back of Wesker's broad trench coat. Once she verified that he was engrossed in his clipboard, she would quickly lift her homemade poster board over her head. Painted in vibrant red glitter were the words. GO ADA! 10/10!

Ada would catch the tiny movement out of the corner of her eye while holding a difficult edge hold. She never broke her form, she never smiled openly, but a tiny, almost invisible flicker of warmth would touch the corners of her eyes before she glided into her next pass.

"Your posture on the entrance curve was slightly delayed," Wesker's low, flat voice echoed across the ice as Ada brought herself to a stop at the barrier. "You are drifting half an inch to the left on the setup edge."

"I can hold the center line tighter," Ada replied smoothly, her breath rising in a soft white mist.

From rows up, Ashley leaned forward over the metal railing, resting her chin on her arms, offering Ada a energetic thumbs-up and a wide, beaming smile.

Wesker didn't turn around, but his voice remained cool and matter-of-fact. "Miss Graham, if you intend to remain in the facility, ensure your poster board does not reflect the overhead lighting into my athlete's line of sight."

Ashley’s eyes widened in mild shock, her cheeks turning bright pink as she quickly pulled the glittery sign down to her lap. "Sorry, Mr. Wesker! Won't happen again!"

Ada turned her face slightly toward the ice to hide the tiny, soft curve touching her lips. "I am ready for the next run, Coach."

"Begin from the far corner," Wesker instructed, clicking his stopwatch. "Incorporate the full choreographic sequence."

The state championship arena two months later was a completely different beast.

The stadium was packed to maximum capacity, a roaring sea of thousands of spectators, flashing press cameras, and echoing public address announcements. The air inside the dome was thick with cold vapor and tense competitive energy. High up in the front row of the official school delegation section, Ashley sat wearing her official crimson team jacket, her hands tightly gripping a fresh banner painted with Ada's name in bold gold lettering.

Down on the ice, the spotlight cut through the dim arena, illuminating the center circle in a brilliant beam of white light.

Ada stood in her mark.

She wore a stunning crimson performance dress that shimmered under the intense arena lamps.To say that she was magnificent was understatement. Ada looked like a creature forged entirely of ice and flame, serene, composed, and utterly untouchable.

In the coach's box near the entrance gate, Wesker stood wrapped in his dark trench coat, his arms crossed over his chest, his dark glasses reflecting the vast stadium lights. He gave her a single, firm nod of his head.

The opening notes of the orchestral music swelled through stadium speakers, filling every corner of the vast arena.

Ada moved.

She sliced across the ice with terrifying, beautiful velocity, her steel blades cutting deep, clean arcs into the pristine surface. Every jump was executed with mathematical perfection. Her triple lutz was high, soaring, and landed with whisper-soft precision. Her combination spins were a blinding vortex of speed and balance.

Then came the climax of her technical program.

Ada built rapid momentum, turning into a deep, sweeping curve along the outer boundary of the rink. She dropped her center of gravity extraordinarily low to the frozen plane, executing a flawless, deep-edge hydroblade. Her body hovered barely two inches above the ice, her left leg extended back in a sleek, unbroken line while her gloved fingertips grazed the cold surface, carving a sharp thin arc across the floor.

The crowd let out a collective, breathless gasp at the sheer physical control required for the move.

Without losing a single mile per hour of speed, Ada rose seamlessly out of the hydroblade, transitioning her weight in a single, fluid movement straight into an exquisite Arabesque glide. She lifted her right leg high behind her, extending her arms gracefully to the sides, her body forming a perfect, breathtaking horizontal line as she sailed effortlessly across the entire length of the rink.

The arena erupted into absolute, thunderous applause.

People rose from their seats, cheering wildly as Ada completed her final combination spin in the center circle, bringing her arms inward before extending them outward to freeze on the final, booming note of the symphony.

Scores flashed across the giant digital screen above the center ice. Technical Score: 99.8. Program Components: 100.0.

The stadium roared. Stuffed animals, red roses, and paper banners rained down from the upper decks onto the ice.

Ada offered a polite, elegant bow to the four corners of the arena, her expression composed, regal, and serene. She glided smoothly off the ice toward the official exit bay, stepping onto the black rubber matting where Wesker stood waiting with her dark warm-up jacket and a clean towel.

"The edge depth on the hydroblade was satisfactory," Wesker noted flatly, draping the jacket over her shoulders with smooth efficiency. "The Arabesque extension met the required angle."

"Thank you, Coach," Ada breathed out, her chest rising and falling in cycles.

Before Wesker could hand her her water bottle, a bright yellow and crimson blur came charging down the restricted athlete corridor.

"ADA!"

Ashley sprinted down the rubber runner, her sneakers squeaking loudly against the floor, her face flushed red with pure, unadulterated joy.

She threw herself directly into Ada's space.

Ashley launched her arms up, wrapping them tightly around Ada's neck, burying her face into the collar of Ada's crimson dress. Her momentum nearly pushed them both back, but Ada caught her instinctively, her arms wrapping securely around Ashley's waist to hold them steady.

"You were amazing!" Ashley squealed, her voice breaking with ecstatic tears as she squeezed Ada with all her strength. "You were so, so amazing! You won! You got first place!"

Ada stood there in the crowded, noisy exit bay, surrounded by flashing cameras and cheering spectators, holding the blonde girl tightly against her chest. A rare, soft, completely genuine smile broke across Ada's face, her dark eyes shining with contentment as she let Ashley cling to her neck like a koala.

Ashley shifted slightly, pulling her head back just enough to press her lips close to the shell of Ada's ear, her warm breath soft against Ada's skin amidst the roaring noise of the arena.

"I love you," Ashley whispered softly, "I love you so much, Ada."

Ada went still for a single, fleeting second, the noise of the stadium fading into a distant, background hum. She didn't pull away. She tightened her arms around Ashley's waist, holding her close, pressing her forehead gently against Ashley's temple in the dim light of the corridor.

"I know, Ashley," Ada murmured back, "I know."

A few feet away, Albert stood with his arms crossed over his chest, his dark glasses reflecting the bright scoreboard displaying her championship win. But he didn't interrupt them. He simply held her warm-up towel as the two girls stood together in the middle of the roaring world.

─•~❉᯽❉~•─

"You have three hours of unassisted apparatus rotation," Wesker had stated calmly. "I expect your beam transitions to be crisp by the time the administrative assembly adjourns. Do not waste time on idle conversations."

"Yes, Coach," Ada had replied smoothly, taking her bag.

Now, an hour into the session, the wide gym floor was alive with activity.

Ada sat atop a four-foot-high stacked leather landing mat near the edge of the auxiliary foam pit. She was dressed in a dark crimson sleeveless training leotard that fit her slender frame perfectly, her dark hair pulled back into a sleek, neat ponytail, her skin glowing with a thin, healthy sheen of sweat from her warm-up drills. Her long, elegant legs swung idly over the side of the thick leather block, her toes naturally pointed in classic classical posture.

Down on the lower blue floor mat, Ashley was attempting to perform a specialized cheerleading trick—a series of rapid back handsprings into a standing illusion split.

"Watch this one, Ada!" Ashley called out, her voice bright, cheerful, and full of boundless energy that seemed to bounce off the high metal beams of the gym ceiling. She wore a bright yellow cropped cheer top and dark shorts, her blonde hair tied up in a high ponytail with a massive red ribbon. "My coach said if I lock my knee on the secondary rotation, I can hold the extension for two full seconds!"

Ashley took a quick breath, launched herself backward across the mat, her hands slapping the blue vinyl with sharp, clean pops, and executed the sequence. She landed on her left foot, sweeping her right leg straight up past her ear in a brilliant, flexible split, holding the balance with her arms extended wide.

Ada sat watching her from the top of the stacked mats, a soft, genuinely fond smile breaking across her usually guarded face. The sight of her girlfriend’s unbridled enthusiasm was a sweet, intoxicating contrast to the rigidworld of competitive metrics she usually inhabited.

"Your hips were slightly misaligned on the takeoff, Ashley," Ada noted softly, her purring voice crossing easily the short distance. "But the final extension was quite graceful."

"It was totally perfect and you know it!" Ashley laughed, wiping a stray lock of blonde hair from her eyes as she trotted over to the base of the stacked mats, looking up at Ada with sparkling blue eyes. "Admit it, you were impressed."

"I am always impressed by your persistence," Ada teased gently, leaning her elbows on her knees as she looked down at her.

Before Ashley could reply, a tall, nervous-looking boy from the boys' regional pommel horse squad timidly approached the edge of their training area. He wore a navy blue team jacket, his hands trembling violently as he held out a massive, elaborate bouquet of fresh red roses and baby's breath wrapped in glossy cellophane. His face was flushed a deep, unnatural shade of red, his eyes wide and brimming with desperate tears.

"A-Ada," the boy stammered out, his voice cracking painfully in the humid air of the gym. "I... I bought these for you. I've watched every single one of your floor routines this season. You are... you are the most amazing person I've ever seen. Please go on a date with me!"

The entire corner of the gym seemed to freeze for a brief, awkward second.

Ashley’s head snapped toward the boy, her bright smile instantly vanishing, replaced by a deep, ferocious glare.

Ada didn't flinch. She remained seated comfortably atop her four-foot leather mat, looking down at the shaking boy with detached gaze.

"That is very generous of you," Ada said smoothly, "However, I must decline. My time is fully committed to my training and my personal life. I cannot accept the flowers."

"P-Please?" the boy squeaked, a single tear actually slipping down his burning cheek as he held the massive bouquet further forward. "Just one dinner? I bought the fancy roses!"

"My answer is no," Ada repeated, her tone remaining soft, civil, and entirely unyielding. "Please return to your apparatus before your coach notices your absence."

The boy let out a small, pathetic squeak, lowered the massive bouquet of roses, turned on his heel, and practically bolted toward the locker rooms, sobbing softly into his jacket sleeve.

Once the boy disappeared around the corner, Ada turned her dark eyes back toward her girlfriend.

Ashley was standing at the base of the mats with her arms tightly crossed over her yellow cheer top. Her lips were pressed into a tight, exaggerated pout, her nose crinkled up in deep annoyance, and she was actively making dramatic, ridiculous facial expressions—puffing out her cheeks, rolling her eyes toward the ceiling, and mimicking the boy's desperate posture with hilarious accuracy.

Ada couldn't help herself. A low chuckle escaped her throat, a genuine sound of pure amusement that echoed softly near the foam pit.

"Are you quite finished making faces, Ashley?" Ada asked, a playful glint dancing in her dark eyes.

"No, I am not finished!" Ashley huffed loudly, turning her back to Ada with an exaggerated, dramatic swivel of her hips. "Fancy roses! 'You're the most amazing person I've ever seen!' Oh, please! He looked like he was going to melt into the floorboards!"

"I rejected him," Ada pointed out.

"You should have thrown a foam block at his head!" Ashley declared, looking over her shoulder with another dramatic eye roll. "He was intruding on our practice time!"

Ada smiled softly, shaking her head. She shifted her weight, sliding down from the top of the tall four-foot leather mat, her bare feet landing with a soft, whisper-light thump against the edge of the safety padding right behind Ashley.

Without a second of hesitation, Ada reached out, her strong, graceful arms wrapping firmly around Ashley’s waist from behind, pulling the blonde girl back flush against her chest.

Ashley let out a dramatic gasp, attempting to keep her grumpy facade intact, but her posture instantly softened against Ada's warm frame. "Hey! Let go! I'm still angry!"

"You are not angry," Ada purred close to her ear, her voice dripping with sweet, playful affection.

Ada reached up with her right hand, her thumb and forefinger gently catching Ashley’s soft cheek, pinching her skin in a tiny, affectionate squishy hold.

"Ow! Ada!" Ashley giggled despite herself, trying to twist her face away while her hands reached back to hold Ada's forearms. "Stop pinching my face! I am attempting to maintain a serious, jealous girlfriend stance here!"

"Your serious stance requires work," Ada whispered, her purring tone full of warmth.

Before Ashley could formulate another playful protest, Ada shifted her balance, tightening her grip around Ashley’s waist. With a swift, smooth, and deliberate push of her legs, Ada leaned backward, sweeping them both directly off the edge of the solid landing floor and straight down into the adjacent pit filled with hundreds of soft, squishy blue foam blocks.

"Whoa!" Ashley shrieked in delight.

They tumbled together into the sea of soft foam cubes, sinking deep into the cushioned abyss with a muffled, violent shower of blue foam blocks bouncing all around them.

Laughter, bright, ringing, and completely genuine, erupted from Ashley’s throat as they settled at the bottom of the deep pit. The soft blocks swallowed them up, insulating them completely from the rest of the noisy gym, creating a tiny, private world buried beneath three feet of foam.

Ada hovered directly over Ashley, her elbows planted firmly into the soft blocks on either side of Ashley’s shoulders to keep her weight off her. Her dark hair had slipped slightly from its ponytail, a few sleek strands framing her flushed, beautiful face. Her dark eyes were shining with a brilliant, rare joy that no sports photographer or journalist had ever managed to capture.

Ashley lay beneath her, her yellow top slightly rumpled, her blonde hair messy, her eyes wide and sparkling with pure love. The dramatic anger was completely gone, replaced by a soft, breathless smile that lit up her entire face.

"You are completely insane, Ada Wong," Ashley giggled softly, her breath warm against Ada's lips as she reached up with her small, soft hands, gently cupping both of Ada's cheeks.

"I am merely efficient at resolving your jealousy," Ada replied smoothly, her low voice vibrating through the tiny space between them.

"I wasn't jealous," Ashley lied softly, her thumbs gently tracing the sharp line of Ada's cheekbones, her eyes filled with absolute tenderness. "I just don't like other people giving you roses. That's my job."

"You haven't given me roses today," Ada noted, a teasing smirk touching her lips.

"I gave you my company," Ashley countered playfully, tugging gently on Ada's face to pull her down. "That is infinitely better than a bunch of silly flowers."

"Unquestionably," Ada murmured.

Ada lowered her face, closing the tiny remaining distance between them. Their lips met in a sweet, lingering, and utterly blissful kiss right there at the bottom of the foam pit. Ashley’s fingers slid up into Ada’s dark hair, holding her close, while Ada leaned into the soft touch, her heart beating in a fast, happy, and entirely unhurried cadence.

They smiled straight through the kiss, their quiet giggles pressing against each other's lips, completely lost in the sweet, simple warmth of being young, safe, and in love.

Suddenly, a familiar voice echoed from the high wooden rafters far above the foam pit. "Ada."

The cool, flat, and distant tone cut through the air like a steel blade.

Both girls went completely still.

High above the edge of the pit, standing on the solid concrete walkway near the apparatus racks, was Wesker. He was clad in his dark trench coat, his arms crossed tightly over his broad chest, his dark glasses reflecting the high halogen lights of the gymnasium.

Ashley gasped, her cheeks turning a bright, brilliant crimson as she quickly released Ada's face, trying frantically to sit up amid the mountain of loose foam blocks. "Mr. Wesker!"

Ada didn't panic. She smoothly pushed herself up into a kneeling position in the foam, brushing a strand of dark hair behind her ear, her face instantly recovering its polite and disciplined composure.

"Coach," Ada replied smoothly, looking up at him from the pit.

Wesker stood looking down into the sea of blue foam blocks at his sixteen-year-old daughter and the cheerleader buried beside her.

"Your three hours of apparatus rotation concluded five minutes ago," Wesker stated flatly, checking his silver pocket watch with a flick of his wrist. "Your balance beam form remains unverified for the afternoon block."

"I am coming, Coach," Ada answered calmly, extending a hand down to help Ashley climb out of the deep foam.

"Clean the foam dust from your training clothes," Wesker instructed, turning his broad shoulders toward the exit doors. "The car is running. We have a forty-minute drive to the ballet academy."

"Yes, Coach," Ada said softly.

Wesker turned and walked toward the glass doors with his long, measured strides, his coat swishing behind him.

Down in the foam pit, Ashley looked at Ada with wide, nervous blue eyes, her cheeks still burning hot. "Do you think he saw us?"

Ada looked at her girlfriend, a tiny, almost invisible smirk touching the corner of her lips as she pulled Ashley securely out of the soft blocks and onto the solid floor mat.

"My father sees everything, Ashley," Ada purred softly, gently fixing the red ribbon in Ashley's blonde hair before picking up her gear bag. "Now come along. We don't want the old man waiting, do we?"

─•~❉᯽❉~•─

The headlights of the sleek black sedan cut two bright, amber tunnels through the dense sea fog, illuminating the empty, wind-swept stretch of coastline where the paved road met the damp sand. Wesker turned off the ignition, the hum of the engine fading into the steady rush of the incoming tide crashing against the shore.

He unbuckled his seatbelt, stepped out into the frigid night air, and unzipped his heavy black leather jacket. He opened the passenger door, unbuckled Ada’s seatbelt, and spread his thick leather jacket wide across the damp, cold sand right beside the front tire.

"Sit," Wesker commanded softly.

Ada didn't question him. She slid gracefully out of the leather seat, resting her slender frame directly onto his jacket to keep her dark warm-up pants from absorbing the ocean moisture. She stretched her long legs straight out over the pale sand, pointing her toes instinctively, letting the cold breeze brush past her cheeks.

Beside her, resting on a flat wooden tray he had brought from the car, was a fresh grease- stained paper bag from the drive-thru. The rich, warm aroma of salty French fries, fried chicken tenders, and toasted buns drifted into the cold night.

Wesker dropped to one knee beside her, setting his dark sunglasses onto the hood of the car before sitting down flat on the sand. He opened the paper bag, pulled out a warm chicken tender, and handed it to her on a crisp paper napkin along with a small plastic cup of sweet plum sauce.

"Your edge control during the second sequence was three degrees off the ideal center line," Wesker stated casually, picking up his own black coffee cup as he looked out at the vast, roaring ocean.

Ada took a neat bite of the warm chicken, chewing slowly before speaking. "The ice near the eastern barrier was soft tonight, Father. The temperature controls in the secondary rink were miscalibrated by two degrees."

"The ice condition is a variable, Ada," Wesker corrected smoothly, his pale eyes tracking the dark, foaming crests of the waves breaking in the distance. "A true champion does not permit variables to dictate the geometry of her landing. You adapt to the surface. You do not ask the surface to adapt to you."

"I adapted," Ada noted,"I held the landing edge for the full four seconds without dropping my shoulder."

"You held it," Wesker agreed,"But you hesitated for a fraction of a microsecond before initiating the takeoff rotation. Why?"

Ada looked down at her pointed toes, her fingers tracing the edge of the paper napkin. "The height required for the triple rotation felt uncertain on the softer edge."

Wesker took a slow sip of his dark coffee, the warm steam rising into the cold night air. He sat beside her, shielding her small body from the bitter ocean wind blowing off the water.

"Uncertainty is a flaw of the mind, not the body," Wesker said, "When you glide toward the takeoff edge, the ice is no longer a solid floor. It is merely a platform for momentum. If you allow fear of the fall to delay your launch, you have already failed the jump before your blades leave the surface."

"Falling on a triple rotation at full speed causes severe structural bruising," Ada pointed out logically, taking a small fry from the paper box. "Caution is a protective mechanism."

"Caution is the luxury of amateur athletes," Wesker countered flatly. "You are not an amateur. You are built for execution. When you commit to the air, you must abandon the desire for safety."

He paused, the ocean waves roaring loudly in the cold space between them. Wesker turned his head slowly, his pale eyes locking onto her dark face with intensity that made the breath catch in her throat.

"I am proud of your progress, Ada," Wesker said softly, "You possess a discipline that very few humans ever achieve. But you must remember one thing."

Ada tilted her head, her dark eyes reflecting the amber glare of the car headlights. "What, Father?"

"Never be scared to take a leap," Wesker instructed, "Whether on the ice, in your studies, or in your life. When the moment arrives to launch yourself into the unknown, you do not look down. You trust your training, you extend your posture, and you take the leap."

Ada looked into his face, feeling the security of his presence. She took a breath, leaning her shoulder lightly against his arm.

"I understand, Father," Ada whispered softly. "I won't be scared."

"Good," Wesker replied flatly, holding open the box of warm fries for her as the cold tide rolled in. "Finish your meal. We have an early conditioning block at six."

The morning sun filtered through the sheer white curtains of the upstairs hallway, casting long, pale boxes of golden light across the polished hardwood floorboards. The house was silent, holding a strange, motionless cold that felt preserved from the night before.

Ada stepped out of her bedroom at seven o'clock sharp.

She was dressed in a soft, light blue baby doll dress, the thin cotton fabric draping loosely around her knees, her feet bare against the cool wood floor. Her dark hair was neatly combed, her red-framed glasses sitting securely on her nose. Her daily schedule dictated a seven-fifteen departure for the morning ballet conservatory, and her sports bag was already packed and waiting near the front entry door.

She walked down the corridor, her bare soles making no sound against the floor.

She stopped outside the mahogany door of Wesker's master bedroom. The door was closed tight, the brass handle cold to the touch.

Ada lifted her hand, her knuckles knocking against the wooden panel with three precise, evenly spaced taps.

"Father," Ada called out smoothly, her voice clear and measured. "It is seven o'clock. The vehicle requires warming before the morning commute."

Silence answered her.

Ada waited for five full seconds, her brow furrowing into a small, subtle line. Albert was never asleep at seven o'clock. He was always downstairs by five-thirty, brewing dark coffee, preparing her breakfast plates, and checking her daily training schedule on his laptop.

She lifted her hand and knocked again, louder this time.

"Father?" Ada called out, her voice dropping its formal cadence, a faint, tiny flicker of uncertainty touching her tone. "We have ballet at seven-thirty."

The house remained completely, unnervingly motionless. No sound of footsteps, no rustle of bedsheets, no low voice answering through the wood.

Ada reached into the pocket of her light blue dress, pulling out the small silver spare key she kept on her keyring. She inserted the key into the brass lock, turned it with a click, and pushed the wooden door open.

The master bedroom was bathed in dim, grey morning light filtering through the drawn blinds. The air inside the room was freezing with strange, sterile stillness that made the tiny hairs on Ada's arms stand straight up.

Lying on the far side of the massive king-sized bed was Wesker.

He was dressed in his dark grey pajamas, lying perfectly flat on his back, his broad hands resting loosely over his stomach, his face turned slightly toward the frosted window pane.

He looked entirely peaceful, too peaceful.

"Father," Ada said, stepping across the threshold, her bare feet pressing into the soft rug. She walked over to the side of the bed, her small frame standing beside his tall, unmoving figure. "Father, wake up. We are going to be late for the conservatory."

Wesker didn't move. He didn't blink. He didn't shift his broad shoulders.

Ada reached out, her slender fingers catching the fabric of his grey pajama sleeve. She shook his arm, gently at first, applying a small amount of firm pressure to his broad shoulder.

"Father?" she called, her voice rising in pitch, the cool composure she had maintained her entire life beginning to fracture around the edges. "Father, please. Wake up."

His body was heavy, stiff, and unnaturally cold beneath her fingertips. His arm moved rigidly under her pull, offering no human resistance, no warm pulse, and no subtle shift in muscle tension.

Panic, hot, sharp, and blinding, flooded through Ada's veins like liquid ice.

She leaned down, placing her hands flat against his broad chest, pressing her ear directly over his heart. She held her breath, her eyes widening in sheer terror as she listened for the familiar thud that had protected her whole existence.

Nothing.

No heartbeat. No air moving through his chest. No warm breath rising against her cheek.
